   *  **Hyperparameter sensitivity:**  The prompt routing relies on reinforcement learning, which in itself can be challenging. The paper shows hyperparameter sensitivity analysis, but it would be helpful to have more guidelines for adapting those parameters.

* **Potential Influence:** This work can significantly influence the field by:
    * **Setting a new benchmark:**  The unified framework and strong results can serve as a new benchmark for urban transportation modeling.
    * **Inspiring new architectures:** The combination of spatiotemporal encoders and adaptive LLM prompting can inspire novel architectures for other domains with structured data.
    * **Promoting multi-task learning:**  The success of the multi-task approach can encourage researchers to explore joint modeling of interconnected tasks.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to urban transportation modeling. The unified framework, learnable prompting mechanism, and strong experimental results demonstrate its potential to address the limitations of existing approaches.  The approach is complex, but the well-defined modular design and the comprehensive experimental validation provide solid support.  The main limitation lies in the reliance on LLMs, which are constantly evolving, and the need for further validation in real-world deployments. Still, the paper makes a strong case for a unified, LLM-based approach to urban transportation tasks and the carefully devised prompt routing is key to the success of the model and a key novelty of this work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Tinker: Diffusion's Gift to 3D--Multi-View Consistent Editing From Sparse Inputs without Per-Scene Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14811v1)
- **Authors**: Canyu Zhao, Xiaoman Li, Tianjian Feng, Zhiyue Zhao, Hao Chen, Chunhua Shen
- **Abstract**: We introduce Tinker, a versatile framework for high-fidelity 3D editing that operates in both one-shot and few-shot regimes without any per-scene finetuning. Unlike prior techniques that demand extensive per-scene optimization to ensure multi-view consistency or to produce dozens of consistent edited input views, Tinker delivers robust, multi-view consistent edits from as few as one or two images. This capability stems from repurposing pretrained diffusion models, which unlocks their latent 3D awareness. To drive research in this space, we curate the first large-scale multi-view editing dataset and data pipeline, spanning diverse scenes and styles. Building on this dataset, we develop our framework capable of generating multi-view consistent edited views without per-scene training, which consists of two novel components: (1) Referring multi-view editor: Enables precise, reference-driven edits that remain coherent across all viewpoints. (2) Any-view-to-video synthesizer: Leverages spatial-temporal priors from video diffusion to perform high-quality scene completion and novel-view generation even from sparse inputs. Through extensive experiments, Tinker significantly reduces the barrier to generalizable 3D content creation, achieving state-of-the-art performance on editing, novel-view synthesis, and rendering enhancement tasks. We believe that Tinker represents a key step towards truly scalable, zero-shot 3D editing. Project webpage: https://aim-uofa.github.io/Tinker
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TINKER: Diffusion's Gift to 3D-Multi-View Consistent Editing From Sparse Inputs Without Per-Scene Optimization":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TINKER, a 3D editing framework designed to produce high-fidelity, multi-view consistent edits from sparse input views (even just one or two images) without requiring per-scene optimization.  TINKER leverages pre-trained diffusion models to unlock their latent 3D awareness.  The framework consists of two main components: 1) a referring multi-view editor that enables precise, reference-driven edits that remain consistent across viewpoints, and 2) an any-view-to-video synthesizer which leverages spatial-temporal priors from video diffusion to perform scene completion and novel view generation from sparse inputs. To facilitate research, the authors also curate a large-scale multi-view editing dataset.  The paper demonstrates state-of-the-art performance on editing, novel-view synthesis, and rendering enhancement tasks, suggesting a step towards truly scalable, zero-shot 3D editing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to 3D editing. Its primary novelty lies in the *elimination of per-scene optimization* and the reliance on a pre-trained diffusion model's inherent 3D understanding. This is a significant departure from previous methods that either necessitate extensive per-scene fine-tuning or require a large number of consistent input views. The two-component framework – the referring editor and the any-view-to-video synthesizer – is also well-designed and innovative. The creation of a large-scale multi-view editing dataset is also a valuable contribution to the field, addressing a current limitation for training and evaluating such methods. Using the "paired" approach (original + edited views) to train the multi-view consistency module is also clever.

**Significance:** The potential significance of this work is high.  By removing the per-scene optimization bottleneck, TINKER makes 3D editing far more accessible and scalable.  It opens the door to more practical applications in content creation, design, and other areas where interactive 3D editing is desired. The emphasis on generalizability (zero-shot) is crucial for widespread adoption. The consistent results shown in various figures, the additional data pipeline, and the overall design provide strong evidence that a user can achieve high-quality results with little effort. The additional applications that the authors point out, such as video reconstruction, are also promising.

**Strengths:**

*   **Per-Scene Optimization Elimination:** This is a major strength, making the approach practical and scalable.
*   **Multi-View Consistency:** The framework effectively maintains consistency across different viewpoints with minimal input.
*   **Data Pipeline:** Creating a large-scale multi-view editing dataset addresses a critical need in the field.
*   **Leveraging Diffusion Models:**  Effectively utilizing pre-trained diffusion models' latent 3D understanding.
*   **Strong Results:** Demonstrating state-of-the-art performance on key 3D editing tasks.
* **Good writing quality**: Paper is well written and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Foundation Model Dependency:** Performance is contingent on the capabilities of the underlying diffusion model.  Limitations in the base model (e.g., inability to handle significant geometric deformations) will translate to limitations in TINKER.
*   **Synthetic Data Bias:**  Training on a synthesized dataset, while necessary, may introduce biases and limit the real-world applicability of the method. The prompts given by the "expert prompt generator" are also potentially problematic, since generating prompts of high-quality automatically is a task in itself.
*   **Limited Geometric Understanding:** Though the paper leverages pre-trained diffusion models which, surprisingly, demonstrate some latent 3D awareness, the scene completion model which relies on depth may not be able to do extreme novel view synthesis.

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of 3D editing. The shift towards a per-scene optimization-free approach, coupled with the creation of a multi-view editing dataset and the demonstrated state-of-the-art performance, justifies a high score. While the limitations related to foundation model dependency and synthetic data bias exist, the strengths significantly outweigh the weaknesses. This work has the potential to influence the direction of future research in 3D editing, making it more accessible and practical. Although there remains potential issues with the geometric understanding of the scene, I believe this work merits a high score because it could lead to some genuinely exciting research directions in the field of zero-shot 3D content generation.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### TransLight: Image-Guided Customized Lighting Control with Generative Decoupling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14814v1)
- **Authors**: Zongming Li, Lianghui Zhu, Haocheng Shen, Longjin Ran, Wenyu Liu, Xinggang Wang
- **Abstract**: Most existing illumination-editing approaches fail to simultaneously provide customized control of light effects and preserve content integrity. This makes them less effective for practical lighting stylization requirements, especially in the challenging task of transferring complex light effects from a reference image to a user-specified target image. To address this problem, we propose TransLight, a novel framework that enables high-fidelity and high-freedom transfer of light effects. Extracting the light effect from the reference image is the most critical and challenging step in our method. The difficulty lies in the complex geometric structure features embedded in light effects that are highly coupled with content in real-world scenarios. To achieve this, we first present Generative Decoupling, where two fine-tuned diffusion models are used to accurately separate image content and light effects, generating a newly curated, million-scale dataset of image-content-light triplets. Then, we employ IC-Light as the generative model and train our model with our triplets, injecting the reference lighting image as an additional conditioning signal. The resulting TransLight model enables customized and natural transfer of diverse light effects. Notably, by thoroughly disentangling light effects from reference images, our generative decoupling strategy endows TransLight with highly flexible illumination control. Experimental results establish TransLight as the first method to successfully transfer light effects across disparate images, delivering more customized illumination control than existing techniques and charting new directions for research in illumination harmonization and editing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TransLight: Image-Guided Customized Lighting Control with Generative Decoupling":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TransLight, a novel method for transferring light effects from a reference image to a target image with customized control. It addresses the limitations of existing illumination editing approaches, which often struggle to provide fine-grained control and preserve content integrity.  TransLight leverages a "Generative Decoupling" strategy, where two fine-tuned diffusion models separate image content and light effects. This decoupled representation is then used to train a generative model (IC-Light) conditioned on the reference lighting, allowing for flexible control over the position, direction, and intensity of the transferred light effects. The authors create a large-scale dataset of image-content-light triplets for training the model. Experimental results demonstrate that TransLight can successfully transfer diverse light effects across disparate images with a high degree of fidelity and freedom.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core novelty of this paper lies in its proposed "Generative Decoupling" approach for isolating light effects from image content and leveraging this decoupled representation for light effect transfer. While style transfer and relighting are established areas, this specific formulation of transferring lighting effects with customized control from a reference image via generative decoupling appears to be a novel contribution. Constructing a dataset of image-content-light triplets is also a significant and practical contribution.
* **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its ability to address a practical problem in image editing with a relatively simple yet powerful approach. The capability to transfer light effects with customized control has numerous applications, including:  photo editing, special effects, and image compositing. The paper shows convincing results that highlight the method's superior performance compared to existing relighting techniques. The Light FID score is an excellent metric for assessing the quality of lighting and provides a tangible demonstration of improvement.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing approaches for image editing.
    * **Novelty and Technical Contribution:** The generative decoupling technique and light effect transfer pipeline are innovative.
    * **Dataset Creation:** The creation of a large-scale dataset of image-content-light triplets is a valuable contribution to the community.
    * **Thorough Evaluation:** The paper presents comprehensive experimental results and comparisons (qualitative and quantitative), demonstrating the effectiveness of the approach.
    * **Customized Control:** TransLight provides flexible adjustment to light effects by allowing translation, flipping/rotating, and scaling of the light effect image.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependence on Light Extraction Model:** The performance of TransLight heavily relies on the effectiveness of the light extraction model. The authors acknowledge this limitation, and performance may degrade when the reference images contain subtle or indistinct light effects.
    * **Diffusion Model Training Overhead:** While the paper provides implementation details, training two separate diffusion models for content/light decoupling is computationally expensive and might limit the accessibility to researchers with fewer resources.
    * **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation primarily focuses on visual quality and quantitative metrics. Exploring user studies might further solidify the benefits of customized control in practical applications.
* **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of image editing by introducing a new framework for light effect manipulation. The proposed generative decoupling approach may inspire future research in disentangled representation learning and controlled image generation. The dataset will likely be used as a benchmark and resource for future models.
* **Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:**

The paper introduces a novel and significant method for transferring lighting effects.  The "Generative Decoupling" idea is innovative, and the results are compelling. The dataset creation is a major strength. However, the dependence on the light extraction model and the computational cost of training two diffusion models prevent it from achieving a higher score. It represents a solid advance over previous approaches, demonstrating improved performance and increased control, however, the practical limitations stemming from model dependency and training expense are significant drawbacks, preventing it from being considered a truly exceptional breakthrough.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Evaluating Retrieval-Augmented Generation vs. Long-Context Input for Clinical Reasoning over EHRs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14817v1)
- **Authors**: Skatje Myers, Dmitriy Dligach, Timothy A. Miller, Samantha Barr, Yanjun Gao, Matthew Churpek, Anoop Mayampurath, Majid Afshar
- **Abstract**: Electronic health records (EHRs) are long, noisy, and often redundant, posing a major challenge for the clinicians who must navigate them. Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising solution for extracting and reasoning over this unstructured text, but the length of clinical notes often exceeds even state-of-the-art models' extended context windows. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) offers an alternative by retrieving task-relevant passages from across the entire EHR, potentially reducing the amount of required input tokens. In this work, we propose three clinical tasks designed to be replicable across health systems with minimal effort: 1) extracting imaging procedures, 2) generating timelines of antibiotic use, and 3) identifying key diagnoses. Using EHRs from actual hospitalized patients, we test three state-of-the-art LLMs with varying amounts of provided context, using either targeted text retrieval or the most recent clinical notes. We find that RAG closely matches or exceeds the performance of using recent notes, and approaches the performance of using the models' full context while requiring drastically fewer input tokens. Our results suggest that RAG remains a competitive and efficient approach even as newer models become capable of handling increasingly longer amounts of text.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) compared to long-context language models (LLMs) for clinical reasoning tasks using Electronic Health Records (EHRs).  The authors define three tasks: extracting imaging procedures, generating antibiotic timelines, and identifying key diagnoses. They compare three LLMs with varying context windows, using both RAG and providing the models with recent notes or long-context inputs. Their findings suggest that RAG consistently performs comparably to or even surpasses using recent notes alone, while requiring significantly fewer input tokens.  The study finds that RAG can approach the performance of using models' full context windows, even when utilizing a fraction of the tokens. The paper emphasizes that RAG remains a competitive and efficient approach, even as models expand their context windows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper's core contribution lies in its systematic comparison of RAG against long-context LLMs *specifically* within the clinical domain using real-world EHR data.  While RAG and long-context models are well-established concepts, their comparative performance in a real-world clinical setting, with tasks designed to reflect actual clinical reasoning, is a valuable contribution. Previous work often focuses on fact extraction from shorter texts or on clinical summarization that doesn't stretch the limits of context windows in realistic clinical cases. They propose a method to allow EHR analysis in any healthcare setting. They also explore different lengths of context windows.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its practical implications for clinical practice.  EHR data is vast and often unwieldy.  Clinicians face the challenge of efficiently extracting relevant information for decision-making.  The paper provides empirical evidence that RAG is a viable solution, potentially reducing computational costs, improving accuracy by filtering noise, and mitigating the "lost-in-the-middle" effect. The identified tasks address real-world clinical reasoning needs. The use of actual hospitalized patient data strengthens the findings' relevance and generalizability. Moreover, they have demonstrated ways to validate their tasks and measure the efficiency of EHR analysis.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined tasks:** The three clinical tasks are clearly defined, clinically relevant, and designed to be replicable across different healthcare systems.
    *   **Rigorous evaluation:**  The study employs appropriate evaluation metrics (F1, Jaccard index) tailored to each task and compares performance across multiple LLMs and context window sizes.
    *   **Real-world data:** The use of real EHR data enhances the study's ecological validity and provides insights into challenges like noise and redundancy.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper has clear implications for improving clinical workflow efficiency and decision support systems.
    *   **Structured Datasets:** The article makes an effort to structure the datatests used for each LLM test.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited dataset release:** The authors acknowledge that they cannot release the dataset due to ethical restrictions, which limits the reproducibility and verification of the study's findings by other researchers. They do, however, provide ample details to reproduce it in a new environment.
    *   **RAG Implementation:** They use a fixed chunking strategy, query formulation, and embedding model, while it will most likely work in a new environment.
    *   **Diagnosis Generation:** They normalize free text with models that are trained to extract medical terms. There may be limitations on their overall accuracy.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in the area of clinical NLP by:
    *   Providing a benchmark for comparing RAG and long-context models on clinical reasoning tasks.
    *   Motivating further exploration of RAG techniques tailored to the specific characteristics of EHR data.
    *   Encouraging the development of more efficient and accurate clinical decision support systems.
    *   Incentivize building more tasks in other medical environments to better analyze patient histories.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by directly comparing RAG and long-context LLMs on clinically relevant tasks using real-world EHR data. The findings provide practical insights into the potential benefits of RAG for improving clinical decision-making and efficiency. The limitations related to the inability to release the dataset and some methodological choices temper the impact slightly. Therefore, a score of 7 is assigned.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Across Languages
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14828v1)
- **Authors**: Josh Barua, Seun Eisape, Kayo Yin, Alane Suhr
- **Abstract**: Scaling inference through long chains-of-thought (CoTs) has unlocked impressive reasoning capabilities in large language models (LLMs), yet the reasoning process remains almost exclusively English-centric. We construct translated versions of two popular English reasoning datasets, fine-tune Qwen 2.5 (7B) and Qwen 3 (8B) models, and present a systematic study of long CoT generation across French, Japanese, Latvian, and Swahili. Our experiments reveal three key findings. First, the efficacy of using English as a pivot language varies by language: it provides no benefit for French, improves performance when used as the reasoning language for Japanese and Latvian, and proves insufficient for Swahili where both task comprehension and reasoning remain poor. Second, extensive multilingual pretraining in Qwen 3 narrows but does not eliminate the cross-lingual performance gap. A lightweight fine-tune using only 1k traces still improves performance by over 30\% in Swahili. Third, data quality versus scale trade-offs are language dependent: small, carefully curated datasets suffice for English and French, whereas larger but noisier corpora prove more effective for Swahili and Latvian. Together, these results clarify when and why long CoTs transfer across languages and provide translated datasets to foster equitable multilingual reasoning research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Long Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Across Languages":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning in multilingual settings, specifically focusing on French, Japanese, Latvian, and Swahili. It challenges the assumption that English can universally serve as a pivot language for CoT reasoning.  The authors fine-tune Qwen models on translated versions of existing English reasoning datasets (s1k and Bespoke-Stratos-17k) and analyze the impact of using English as a pivot, the role of multilingual pretraining, and the data quality versus scale trade-offs for different languages.  Key findings include: (1) the efficacy of English pivoting varies across languages; (2) extensive multilingual pretraining narrows the performance gap, but fine-tuning is still beneficial, especially for low-resource languages; and (3) data quality/quantity requirements for fine-tuning depend on the language. The paper also provides translated datasets to encourage further multilingual reasoning research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's primary novelty lies in its systematic multilingual analysis of *long* chain-of-thought reasoning, a crucial distinction from prior work mostly focused on shorter CoTs. While previous studies explored multilingual reasoning and English as a pivot language, this paper offers a more granular view by examining different language families and resource levels.  The investigation into the interplay between multilingual pretraining and fine-tuning specifically for *long* CoT in diverse languages is a valuable addition. The translated datasets themselves contribute to the community, although the translation methodology (using Gemini 2.0) isn't groundbreaking, it is a practical approach to address data scarcity.
**Significance:**

The paper addresses an important limitation of current LLM research, which is the overwhelming focus on English. By demonstrating that English is not a universally effective pivot language and that data requirements vary across languages, the paper provides crucial insights for building more equitable and accessible reasoning systems. The work is timely, given the increasing emphasis on multilingual LLMs. The findings that both data size vs quality has different effects on performance based on language resource constraints is important.
**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Experimentation:** The paper presents a well-designed experimental setup with clear baselines and controlled variables. The selection of languages representing diverse language families is a strength.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The findings offer practical guidance for researchers and practitioners working on multilingual LLMs, highlighting the need for language-specific fine-tuning and careful consideration of data quality and quantity.
*   **Dataset Contribution:** The released datasets will facilitate future research on multilingual CoT reasoning.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and easy to follow, with clear explanations of the methodology and results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Translation Quality Concerns:** While Gemini 2.0 is a strong translation model, automatically translated datasets may still contain errors or biases that could affect the results. Human verification or iterative refinement of the translated data could further strengthen the study.
*   **Limited Model Scope:** Focusing on Qwen models only, while practical, limits the generalizability of the findings. Exploring other LLM architectures and sizes would be valuable. It could also explore a bit more in-depth the linguistic characteristics that lead to the results such as a cross-lingual transferability metric.
*   **Narrow Evaluation Benchmarks:** While MATH-500, AIME, IMO and GPQA are valuable, the evaluation is primarily focused on mathematics and STEM. Expanding the evaluation to include reasoning tasks from other domains, like commonsense reasoning or reading comprehension, would provide a more comprehensive assessment.
*   **Deeper Linguistic Analysis could be added**: While the paper provides a breakdown of languages, it fails to deeply discuss the linguistic aspects.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by systematically investigating long CoT reasoning across languages and highlighting the limitations of English-centric approaches. The findings have practical implications for building more equitable and accessible LLMs, and the released datasets will encourage further research. The weaknesses related to data quality concerns, model scope, evaluation benchmarks, and deeper exploration of the language aspects do temper the overall impact.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper is novel and addresses an important problem in the field of multilingual LLMs. The experimentation is systematic, and the findings are actionable. However, the limitations, particularly related to relying solely on translated data and limited exploration of a diverse set of models, impact the overall significance and justify a score of 7 rather than a higher one. A more detailed linguistic analysis of the results could have elevated the score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Universal and Transferable Adversarial Attack on Large Language Models Using Exponentiated Gradient Descent
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14853v1)
- **Authors**: Sajib Biswas, Mao Nishino, Samuel Jacob Chacko, Xiuwen Liu
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in critical applications, ensuring their robustness and safety alignment remains a major challenge. Despite the overall success of alignment techniques such as reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF) on typical prompts, LLMs remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks enabled by crafted adversarial triggers appended to user prompts. Most existing jailbreak methods either rely on inefficient searches over discrete token spaces or direct optimization of continuous embeddings. While continuous embeddings can be given directly to selected open-source models as input, doing so is not feasible for proprietary models. On the other hand, projecting these embeddings back into valid discrete tokens introduces additional complexity and often reduces attack effectiveness. We propose an intrinsic optimization method which directly optimizes relaxed one-hot encodings of the adversarial suffix tokens using exponentiated gradient descent coupled with Bregman projection, ensuring that the optimized one-hot encoding of each token always remains within the probability simplex. We provide theoretical proof of convergence for our proposed method and implement an efficient algorithm that effectively jailbreaks several widely used LLMs. Our method achieves higher success rates and faster convergence compared to three state-of-the-art baselines, evaluated on five open-source LLMs and four adversarial behavior datasets curated for evaluating jailbreak methods. In addition to individual prompt attacks, we also generate universal adversarial suffixes effective across multiple prompts and demonstrate transferability of optimized suffixes to different LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel adversarial attack method for jailbreaking Large Language Models (LLMs).  The approach uses exponentiated gradient descent (EGD) to optimize relaxed one-hot token encodings for adversarial suffixes, effectively crafting triggers that bypass LLM safety mechanisms.  The method intrinsically enforces constraints on the one-hot encodings during optimization, avoiding the need for explicit projection steps common in prior work.  The authors provide a theoretical convergence guarantee for their method and demonstrate empirically that it achieves higher success rates and faster convergence compared to state-of-the-art baselines on several open-source LLMs and a variety of adversarial behavior datasets.  Furthermore, they generate universal adversarial suffixes that generalize across multiple prompts and demonstrate transferability to different LLM architectures, including proprietary models like GPT-3.5.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The primary novelty lies in the use of EGD with relaxed one-hot encodings for adversarial suffix optimization in LLMs. While EGD itself is not new, its application in this context, specifically addressing the challenges of discrete token space manipulation without explicit projection steps, constitutes a significant contribution.  The theoretical convergence proof, while limited to specific model types, strengthens the theoretical foundation. The generation and demonstration of universal, transferable adversarial suffixes also builds upon existing work but provides a practical advancement in attack methodology. Prior work using PGD relies on projection. EGD handles these constraints.

**Significance:** The work is significant for several reasons:

*   **Improved Attack Effectiveness:** The method demonstrably outperforms existing baselines in terms of success rate and convergence speed, indicating a practical advancement in LLM jailbreaking techniques.  This is valuable for understanding LLM vulnerabilities and informing defensive strategies.
*   **Broad Applicability:** The method is evaluated on multiple LLMs and datasets, demonstrating its generalizability. The demonstration of transferability to proprietary models like GPT-3.5 highlights the potential real-world impact.
*   **Theoretical Foundation:** The theoretical convergence proof provides a stronger basis for the method compared to purely empirical approaches.
*   **Understanding LLM Vulnerabilities:** The work contributes to a deeper understanding of LLM vulnerabilities to adversarial prompting and the limitations of current safety alignment techniques.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope of Convergence Proof:** The theoretical convergence guarantee only applies to specific smooth models and does not extend to the Adam variant of EGD used in practice. This weakens the theoretical grounding.
*   **White-Box Setting:** The method relies on full access to model weights, limiting its applicability to open-source LLMs.  Its viability in black-box scenarios is not demonstrated, although future research is stated as a goal.  This is a significant limitation in practical terms, as many deployed LLMs are proprietary.
*   **Transferability Limitations:** The transferability results show limited success on more recent models like GPT-4o-mini and Claude, suggesting that newer alignment techniques may offer increased resistance to transfer-based attacks.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** While acknowledged, the potential for misuse of the presented techniques raises ethical concerns. Although the authors emphasize responsible use, the release of such methods inherently presents risks.

**Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence research in several ways:

*   **Motivate Development of Robust Defenses:**  The demonstrated effectiveness of the attack method will likely spur research into more robust defense mechanisms against adversarial prompting.
*   **Inform Model Alignment Strategies:**  The findings can inform the development of better safety alignment techniques that are less susceptible to bypass through adversarial manipulation.
*   **Advance Understanding of LLM Security:** The work contributes to a growing body of literature focused on understanding and mitigating the security vulnerabilities of LLMs.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper provides a solid contribution to the field of LLM security, with a novel method that demonstrably outperforms existing baselines. The theoretical foundation and empirical evaluation are reasonably strong. However, the limitations regarding the scope of the convergence proof, the white-box setting, and the observed limitations in transferability to more recent LLMs detract from its overall impact. The ethical concerns, inherent in this type of research, further necessitate a measured assessment.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Prompting Brain: Neurocognitive Markers of Expertise in Guiding Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14869v1)
- **Authors**: Hend Al-Khalifa, Raneem Almansour, Layan Abdulrahman Alhuasini, Alanood Alsaleh, Mohamad-Hani Temsah, Mohamad-Hani_Temsah, Ashwag Rafea S Alruwaili
- **Abstract**: Prompt engineering has rapidly emerged as a critical skill for effective interaction with large language models (LLMs). However, the cognitive and neural underpinnings of this expertise remain largely unexplored. This paper presents findings from a cross-sectional pilot fMRI study investigating differences in brain functional connectivity and network activity between experts and intermediate prompt engineers. Our results reveal distinct neural signatures associated with higher prompt engineering literacy, including increased functional connectivity in brain regions such as the left middle temporal gyrus and the left frontal pole, as well as altered power-frequency dynamics in key cognitive networks. These findings offer initial insights into the neurobiological basis of prompt engineering proficiency. We discuss the implications of these neurocognitive markers in Natural Language Processing (NLP). Understanding the neural basis of human expertise in interacting with LLMs can inform the design of more intuitive human-AI interfaces, contribute to cognitive models of LLM interaction, and potentially guide the development of AI systems that better align with human cognitive workflows. This interdisciplinary approach aims to bridge the gap between human cognition and machine intelligence, fostering a deeper understanding of how humans learn and adapt to complex AI systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the neurocognitive basis of prompt engineering expertise in large language models (LLMs) using fMRI. It compares brain activity and connectivity between expert and intermediate prompt engineers. The study finds distinct neural signatures associated with higher prompt engineering literacy, including increased functional connectivity in the left middle temporal gyrus and left frontal pole, as well as differences in power-frequency dynamics within key cognitive networks (visual, default mode, and left lateral parietal). The authors discuss the implications for NLP and HCI, suggesting that understanding these neural underpinnings can inform the design of more intuitive human-AI interfaces and training programs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in its novelty. While research exists on prompt engineering techniques and the cognitive aspects of AI interaction, this appears to be the first study to directly investigate the neural correlates of prompt engineering expertise using fMRI. This is a significant step towards understanding the human element in LLM interaction.

*   **Significance:** The findings have potentially important implications for the NLP and HCI fields. Identifying neural markers of expertise can inform the design of more effective training programs for prompt engineering, as well as the development of AI systems that better align with human cognitive processes. By understanding which brain regions are most active during effective prompting, interface designs can be tailored to reduce cognitive load and enhance user performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novel research question:** Addresses a gap in understanding the human cognitive processes involved in prompt engineering.
    *   **Rigorous methodology:** Employs fMRI to provide empirical evidence of neural differences between expert and intermediate prompt engineers.
    *   **Interdisciplinary approach:** Bridges insights from neuroscience, cognitive science, NLP, and HCI.
    *   **Clear and well-articulated implications:** Discusses the potential applications of the findings for interface design and training programs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Small sample size:** The study's sample size of 22 participants limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Cross-sectional design:** The cross-sectional design does not allow for causal inferences. It's unclear whether the observed neural patterns are a cause or a consequence of expertise.
    *   **Exploratory PELS Cutoff:** The criteria to divide expert and intermediate relies on a potentially overfitted cutoff. The study should have explored the impact of different cutoff points on the data.
    *   **Resting-state fMRI:** The use of resting-state fMRI limits the ability to directly link neural patterns to specific cognitive processes involved in prompt engineering. Task-based fMRI studies would provide more direct evidence.
    *   **Limited validation:** The findings are based on preliminary data and require replication with larger and more diverse samples.
    *   **Overstated Implications:** The paper's implications lean toward potentially promising, yet speculative avenues. The paper could be more conservative and highlight the exploratory nature of its findings.

*   **Potential Influence:** This study could stimulate further research in the area of neurocognitive aspects of AI interaction, leading to more human-centered AI systems and training methodologies.

*   **Overall:** Despite the limitations related to sample size, study design, and preliminary nature of the findings, the paper addresses a novel and significant research question. It offers a valuable first step toward understanding the neural basis of prompt engineering expertise and has the potential to influence the design of more intuitive human-AI interfaces and training programs. However, the small sample size, design limitations, and high possibility of overstated implications reduce the paper's overall impact at this stage.

### Squeezed Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14871v1)
- **Authors**: Jyotirmai Singh, Samar Khanna, James Burgess
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models typically inject isotropic Gaussian noise, disregarding structure in the data. Motivated by the way quantum squeezed states redistribute uncertainty according to the Heisenberg uncertainty principle, we introduce Squeezed Diffusion Models (SDM), which scale noise anisotropically along the principal component of the training distribution. As squeezing enhances the signal-to-noise ratio in physics, we hypothesize that scaling noise in a data-dependent manner can better assist diffusion models in learning important data features. We study two configurations: (i) a Heisenberg diffusion model that compensates the scaling on the principal axis with inverse scaling on orthogonal directions and (ii) a standard SDM variant that scales only the principal axis. Counterintuitively, on CIFAR-10/100 and CelebA-64, mild antisqueezing - i.e. increasing variance on the principal axis - consistently improves FID by up to 15% and shifts the precision-recall frontier toward higher recall. Our results demonstrate that simple, data-aware noise shaping can deliver robust generative gains without architectural changes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Squeezed Diffusion Models (SDM), a novel approach to enhance diffusion models by anisotropically scaling noise along the principal component of the training data distribution.  This is inspired by quantum squeezing techniques which redistribute noise to boost signal-to-noise ratio.  The authors explore two configurations: a Heisenberg diffusion model that conserves uncertainty by counter-scaling noise in orthogonal directions and a standard SDM that only scales noise along the principal axis.  Surprisingly, the experiments on CIFAR-10/100 and CelebA-64 demonstrate that mild *antisqueezing* (increasing noise along the principal axis) consistently improves FID and shifts the precision-recall frontier, suggesting that data-aware noise shaping can lead to improved generative performance without complex architectural modifications.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces a relatively simple yet intriguing idea: adapting the noise injection process in diffusion models based on the principal components of the data. The analogy to quantum squeezing is interesting, even if the underlying theoretical connection isn't rigorously established.  The experimental finding that *antisqueezing* works best is counterintuitive, adding to the novelty. Using PCA to align the squeezing directions is also a relatively standard but simple trick that simplifies computation. However, the core idea of data-dependent noise scaling, while not widespread, has been explored in previous works [4, 5], somewhat diminishing the novelty of this paper.

*   **Significance:** The paper demonstrates that altering the noise structure in a data-aware manner can lead to significant improvements in diffusion models without architectural changes.  The fact that antisqueezing works better than squeezing suggests that a higher SNR might not always be the best objective for generative models. This provides useful insights for future research in noise scheduling and data-dependent noise adaptation. Furthermore, the paper provides a simple and easily implementable method to improve the performance of existing diffusion models. The gains are significant (up to 15% FID improvement). However, the method's dependence on PCA limits its applicability to very high-dimensional data where PCA becomes computationally expensive. Furthermore, the technique is only tested on relatively low resolution image datasets.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-written.
    *   Simple and easy to implement.
    *   Significant empirical improvements on standard datasets.
    *   Interesting counterintuitive finding (antisqueezing).
    *   Good ablation studies exploring different model variants and datasets.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Theoretical justification for the antisqueezing results is lacking.  The authors provide no clear explanation for *why* it works better.
    *   Limited applicability to high-dimensional data due to PCA bottleneck.
    *   Experiments limited to relatively low-resolution image datasets (CIFAR, CelebA-64).
    *   The related work could better acknowledge and contextualize related work on adaptive noise processes in diffusion models. The connection to quantum squeezing is largely a heuristic, and could be elaborated on with more precision.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research on data-aware noise scheduling in diffusion models. It might encourage the development of more sophisticated methods for adapting the noise structure based on data characteristics. The counterintuitive finding about antisqueezing could lead to a re-evaluation of the role of SNR in generative models.

**Score: 6**

*Rationale:* The paper presents a novel and practically useful method for improving diffusion models with a simple twist on the noise process. While the concept is interesting and empirically effective, the lack of a strong theoretical explanation for the findings, the limited applicability to high-dimensional data, and the restricted scale of the experiments (low res image datasets) prevent it from achieving a higher score. Furthermore, despite drawing an analogy with quantum squeezing, the practical contribution of this paper is fairly incremental, and the main outcome hinges on the empirical observation that increasing noise in a data-aware manner can improve performance. Given the prior art on adaptive noise processes for diffusion models, the novelty is not particularly groundbreaking. Still, the method is effective and offers valuable insight into the role of noise in diffusion model training, making it a worthwhile contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Quantization Meets dLLMs: A Systematic Study of Post-training Quantization for Diffusion LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14896v1)
- **Authors**: Haokun Lin, Haobo Xu, Yichen Wu, Ziyu Guo, Renrui Zhang, Zhichao Lu, Ying Wei, Qingfu Zhang, Zhenan Sun
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have introduced a promising alternative to autoregressive (AR) LLMs for natural language generation tasks, leveraging full attention and denoising-based decoding strategies. However, the deployment of these models on edge devices remains challenging due to their massive parameter scale and high resource demands. While post-training quantization (PTQ) has emerged as a widely adopted technique for compressing AR LLMs, its applicability to dLLMs remains largely unexplored. In this work, we present the first systematic study on quantizing diffusion-based language models. We begin by identifying the presence of activation outliers, characterized by abnormally large activation values that dominate the dynamic range. These outliers pose a key challenge to low-bit quantization, as they make it difficult to preserve precision for the majority of values. More importantly, we implement state-of-the-art PTQ methods and conduct a comprehensive evaluation across multiple task types and model variants. Our analysis is structured along four key dimensions: bit-width, quantization method, task category, and model type. Through this multi-perspective evaluation, we offer practical insights into the quantization behavior of dLLMs under different configurations. We hope our findings provide a foundation for future research in efficient dLLM deployment. All codes and experimental setups will be released to support the community.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents the first systematic study of applying post-training quantization (PTQ) to diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs). It identifies the presence of activation outliers in dLLMs, which pose a challenge for low-bit quantization. The authors implement state-of-the-art PTQ methods (GPTQ, AWQ, SmoothQuant, QuaRot, and DuQuant) and comprehensively evaluate their performance across different bit-widths, quantization methods, task categories (QA, math, code), and dLLM model types (LLaDA-Base, LLaDA-Instruct, Dream-7B). The analysis reveals that 4-bit quantization is generally effective for weight-only quantization, while 8-bit is preferable for weight-activation quantization. GPTQ tends to outperform AWQ, while rotation-based weight-activation quantization (QuaRot, DuQuant) excels. The paper highlights that math and code tasks are more sensitive to quantization, and instruction-tuned models are more robust to quantization than base models. The paper concludes with practical recommendations for quantizing dLLMs and suggests avenues for future research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in being the *first* systematic study of PTQ specifically for *diffusion-based* LLMs. While PTQ for autoregressive LLMs is well-established, its application to dLLMs is a relatively unexplored area. The characterization of activation outliers in dLLMs and the investigation of their impact on quantization are valuable contributions.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial challenge: efficient deployment of dLLMs. By demonstrating the feasibility of PTQ for dLLMs and providing practical guidelines, the paper contributes to making these models more accessible and deployable on resource-constrained devices.  The empirical analysis across various tasks and model types is significant for guiding future research in this area.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a very thorough experimental evaluation, covering a wide range of PTQ methods, bit-widths, tasks, and model types.
    *   **Practical Insights:** The findings offer actionable recommendations for quantizing dLLMs, such as the preferred bit-widths and effective quantization methods.
    *   **Identification of Challenges:** The paper clearly highlights the challenges posed by activation outliers and task-specific sensitivity to quantization.
    *   **Code Release:** The promise of releasing code and experimental setups significantly enhances the reproducibility and impact of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While comprehensive in scope, the paper could benefit from a deeper analysis of *why* certain methods perform better than others for specific tasks or models. The hypotheses presented are somewhat shallow and could be supported by more detailed analyses of activation distributions, quantization error propagation, or model architecture characteristics.
    *   **Incremental Contribution (somewhat):** Although the *application* to dLLMs is novel, many of the methods used (GPTQ, AWQ, etc.) are borrowed from the autoregressive LLM quantization literature. The novelty comes mainly from showing *how well* (or not) those existing methods transfer.
    *   **Lack of New Quantization Techniques:** The paper primarily evaluates existing methods rather than proposing a fundamentally new quantization technique tailored to the specific characteristics of dLLMs.

*   **Impact:** The paper is likely to have a moderate impact on the field. It provides a valuable starting point for researchers and practitioners interested in quantizing dLLMs. The findings will inform future research directions and potentially lead to the development of more efficient and robust quantization techniques specifically designed for dLLMs. The code release will further facilitate adoption and experimentation.

* **Justification for the Score:** The paper addresses an important, albeit relatively narrow, gap in the literature. While comprehensive, the research relies on existing techniques with incremental contribution, especially through its thoroughness of empirical evaluations and the practicality it provides. This paper is more valuable practically, while the novelty lacks depth.

### Improving LLMs for Machine Translation Using Synthetic Preference Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14951v1)
- **Authors**: Dario Vajda, Domen Vreš, Marko Robnik-Šikonja
- **Abstract**: Large language models have emerged as effective machine translation systems. In this paper, we explore how a general instruction-tuned large language model can be improved for machine translation using relatively few easily produced data resources. Using Slovene as a use case, we improve the GaMS-9B-Instruct model using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) training on a programmatically curated and enhanced subset of a public dataset. As DPO requires pairs of quality-ranked instances, we generated its training dataset by translating English Wikipedia articles using two LLMs, GaMS-9B-Instruct and EuroLLM-9B-Instruct. We ranked the resulting translations based on heuristics coupled with automatic evaluation metrics such as COMET. The evaluation shows that our fine-tuned model outperforms both models involved in the dataset generation. In comparison to the baseline models, the fine-tuned model achieved a COMET score gain of around 0.04 and 0.02, respectively, on translating Wikipedia articles. It also more consistently avoids language and formatting errors.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a method for improving English-to-Slovene machine translation using Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) on a large language model (LLM), GaMS-9B-Instruct.  Recognizing the scarcity of high-quality Slovene translation data and, particularly, the lack of preference-annotated data, the authors develop a synthetic dataset. This dataset is created by translating English Wikipedia and news articles using two different LLMs (GaMS-9B-Instruct and EuroLLM-9B-Instruct). Translations are then ranked using a combination of heuristic rules to identify obvious errors (e.g., wrong language, truncation, stylistic formatting), and the COMET metric for fluency. The resulting preference data is used to fine-tune the GaMS-9B-Instruct model with DPO. The fine-tuned model demonstrates improved performance on SloBench and on unseen Wikipedia and CC-News articles compared to the original model and the other LLM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the combination of synthetic data generation, heuristic error detection, and DPO fine-tuning to enhance translation capabilities for a less-resourced language. The approach of programmatically generating preference data by leveraging the (dis)agreement of two LLMs and automatic metrics is innovative. While synthetic data generation for translation is not entirely new, the use of *preference data* generated in this way for DPO is less common.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the fact that the methodology provides a practical way to enhance machine translation for languages with limited resources.  The resulting open-source model could benefit the Slovene NLP community. The modular pipeline consisting of synthetic data creation, heuristic based filtering and preference pair curation is also significant as it allows for creation of task specific datasets. The demonstrated improvements on the custom datasets are meaningful.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Approach:** The method offers a concrete solution to the problem of data scarcity for less-resourced languages.
    *   **Clear Methodology:**  The data generation, filtering, and DPO fine-tuning steps are well-explained, facilitating reproducibility.  The hyperparameter search is also documented.
    *   **Quantitative Evaluation:**  The model is rigorously evaluated on multiple benchmarks (SloBench, custom Wikipedia, CC-News), using both automatic metrics and checks for common errors.
    *   **Open-Source:** The trained model and data generation code are publicly available, fostering further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Domain:** The training data primarily focuses on Wikipedia and news articles, which might limit the model's performance in other domains (as acknowledged by the authors). The method does not account for any domain specific biases that may occur during synthetic dataset creation, making it hard to evaluate the performance on domains other than those trained on.
    *   **Synthetic Data Biases:** While the authors leverage two LLMs, there is no guarantee that the "better" output of the pair is actually perfect. The reliance on the COMET metric introduces a potential bias towards its inherent strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Heuristic Limitations:** The heuristic error detection is clever, but may not capture all types of translation errors. The threshold for identifying errors such as truncations may require tuning depending on domain of training data.
    *   **Scalability of the Heuristics based data filtering method** The paper assumes that filtering techniques would be scalable for larger domain specific datasets. Since no evidence is provided, the method may need tuning for scalability of the data filtering module.
*   **Impact:** The paper provides a valuable case study of how to improve machine translation for less-resourced languages. The approach could inspire similar work for other languages and tasks where labeled data is scarce. The results could guide future research on synthetic data generation and DPO for NLP tasks.

**Justification:** The work provides a practical and effective methodology for improving machine translation in low-resource settings through preference based fine tuning. The key innovation of synthetic data generation makes it practical to apply similar methods across various NLP tasks. However, there exist several limitations to the method in terms of domain applicability and scalability of data curation methods. Considering the paper's novel approach to synthetic data generation, the modular and repeatable training pipeline and improvements on existing baselines, I would rate the paper as follows.

### Aura-CAPTCHA: A Reinforcement Learning and GAN-Enhanced Multi-Modal CAPTCHA System
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14976v1)
- **Authors**: Joydeep Chandra, Prabal Manhas, Ramanjot Kaur, Rashi Sahay
- **Abstract**: Aura-CAPTCHA was developed as a multi-modal CAPTCHA system to address vulnerabilities in traditional methods that are increasingly bypassed by AI technologies, such as Optical Character Recognition (OCR) and adversarial image processing. The design integrated Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) for generating dynamic image challenges, Reinforcement Learning (RL) for adaptive difficulty tuning, and Large Language Models (LLMs) for creating text and audio prompts. Visual challenges included 3x3 grid selections with at least three correct images, while audio challenges combined randomized numbers and words into a single task. RL adjusted difficulty based on incorrect attempts, response time, and suspicious user behavior. Evaluations on real-world traffic demonstrated a 92% human success rate and a 10% bot bypass rate, significantly outperforming existing CAPTCHA systems. The system provided a robust and scalable approach for securing online applications while remaining accessible to users, addressing gaps highlighted in previous research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Aura-CAPTCHA, a multi-modal CAPTCHA system designed to overcome the limitations of traditional CAPTCHAs that are increasingly vulnerable to AI-powered bots. Aura-CAPTCHA utilizes Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) for generating dynamic image challenges, Reinforcement Learning (RL) for adapting challenge difficulty in real-time based on user behavior, and Large Language Models (LLMs) for generating text and audio prompts. The system presents users with synchronized audio-visual challenges, such as selecting correct images from a grid and transcribing audio. Experiments on real-world traffic show improved human success rates and lower bot bypass rates compared to existing CAPTCHA systems. The authors claim their system is more secure, adaptable, and user-friendly.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Multi-Modal Approach:** The combination of audio and visual elements is a well-established technique for improving CAPTCHA robustness. Aura-CAPTCHA builds on this by synchronizing these modalities.
    *   **Adaptive Difficulty with RL:** The use of Reinforcement Learning to dynamically adjust the CAPTCHA difficulty based on user interaction is a significant improvement over static CAPTCHA designs. It potentially balances security and usability.
    *   **Generative Content:** Using GANs to create dynamic and unpredictable challenges can hinder AI-based solvers that rely on pattern recognition.
    *   **Performance Evaluation:** The paper presents experimental results that demonstrate the system's effectiveness in terms of bypass rate, human success rate, and response time. The comparative analysis against traditional CAPTCHAs strengthens the claims of improvement.
    *   **User Interaction Analysis:** Incorporating user interaction data (e.g., mouse movements, clicks) further enhances bot detection capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty:** While Aura-CAPTCHA combines several existing techniques (GANs, RL, multi-modality), the novelty lies primarily in the specific integration and parameter tuning. This incremental improvement doesn't represent a radical departure from the state-of-the-art.
    *   **Evaluation Rigor:** While metrics are presented, the details of the experiment setup are suppressed due to length constraints.  Information about the size and diversity of both the human user and bot datasets is absent. A clear description of the bot attacks used to test the system are omitted. Without these details, the validity of the evaluation is difficult to assess. The improvement over existing systems may depend heavily on the specific bots tested.
    *   **Accessibility Concerns:** Multi-modal systems can pose challenges for users with disabilities. While the abstract mentions addressing accessibility gaps, the paper lacks specific details on how Aura-CAPTCHA mitigates these concerns. This undermines the claim of enhanced accessibility.
    *   **Scalability:** GANs and RL models can be computationally expensive.  The paper doesn't address the potential scalability challenges of deploying Aura-CAPTCHA on high-traffic websites.
    *   **Generalizability:** The system's effectiveness may depend on the specific characteristics of the target user population and the evolving AI attack landscape.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The integration of RL for adaptive difficulty tuning in CAPTCHA systems is a valuable contribution that could influence future CAPTCHA designs.
    *   The paper provides a practical demonstration of how GANs can be used to generate dynamic CAPTCHA challenges.
    *   The evaluation results contribute to the ongoing effort to develop more secure and user-friendly CAPTCHA systems.

**Justification for Score:**

Aura-CAPTCHA represents a solid engineering effort that combines several existing techniques to improve CAPTCHA security and usability.  However, the lack of detailed information regarding the bots it was evaluated against along with the scalability of the approach significantly diminishes the quality of the paper. While the system shows promising results, its overall impact on the field is likely to be incremental rather than revolutionary.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Multilingual Datasets for Custom Input Extraction and Explanation Requests Parsing in Conversational XAI Systems
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14982v1)
- **Authors**: Qianli Wang, Tatiana Anikina, Nils Feldhus, Simon Ostermann, Fedor Splitt, Jiaao Li, Yoana Tsoneva, Sebastian Möller, Vera Schmitt
- **Abstract**: Conversational explainable artificial intelligence (ConvXAI) systems based on large language models (LLMs) have garnered considerable attention for their ability to enhance user comprehension through dialogue-based explanations. Current ConvXAI systems often are based on intent recognition to accurately identify the user's desired intention and map it to an explainability method. While such methods offer great precision and reliability in discerning users' underlying intentions for English, a significant challenge in the scarcity of training data persists, which impedes multilingual generalization. Besides, the support for free-form custom inputs, which are user-defined data distinct from pre-configured dataset instances, remains largely limited. To bridge these gaps, we first introduce MultiCoXQL, a multilingual extension of the CoXQL dataset spanning five typologically diverse languages, including one low-resource language. Subsequently, we propose a new parsing approach aimed at enhancing multilingual parsing performance, and evaluate three LLMs on MultiCoXQL using various parsing strategies. Furthermore, we present Compass, a new multilingual dataset designed for custom input extraction in ConvXAI systems, encompassing 11 intents across the same five languages as MultiCoXQL. We conduct monolingual, cross-lingual, and multilingual evaluations on Compass, employing three LLMs of varying sizes alongside BERT-type models.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can help you with that! Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on novelty and significance:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the need for multilingual datasets and parsing approaches to improve Conversational Explainable AI (ConvXAI) systems. It introduces two new datasets:

1.  **MultiCoXQL:** A multilingual extension of the existing CoXQL dataset, spanning five typologically diverse languages (English, German, Russian, Chinese, and Telugu). This dataset is created by translating the English CoXQL data while preserving intent annotations.
2.  **Compass:** A new multilingual dataset designed for custom input extraction in ConvXAI systems, covering the same five languages and encompassing 11 intents. Compass includes user questions, extracted custom inputs, and corresponding intent annotations.

The paper also proposes a new parsing approach called Guided Multi-prompt Parsing (GMP), designed to enhance multilingual parsing performance by combining existing methods. The paper then evaluates various Large Language Models (LLMs) and BERT-type models on both datasets, assessing their performance in monolingual, cross-lingual, and multilingual settings using different parsing strategies and information extraction techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   **Datasets:** The creation of MultiCoXQL and Compass datasets represents a valuable contribution.  While machine translation has been used to create multilingual datasets before, adapting and curating it for the specific domain of ConvXAI is novel. The Compass dataset is particularly interesting because it targets the less-explored area of custom input extraction. Prior datasets in ConvXAI have focused on pre-defined data points, so the introduction of a dataset tailored for free-form user input is a significant step.
    *   **Parsing Approach:** The Guided Multi-prompt Parsing (GMP) approach has *some* novelty. It builds upon existing methods like Guided Decoding and Multi-prompt Parsing but combines them in a way that aims to leverage their respective strengths for multilingual scenarios. The integration of SBERT for intent centroid computation and dynamic prompt construction seems like a logical progression.  However, the core ideas aren't revolutionary.
    *   **Overall:** The novelty lies more in the **application** of existing techniques to a specific, less-explored problem (multilingual ConvXAI with custom input) and the creation of resources to facilitate this research.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Addressing Data Scarcity:** The primary significance of this work is that it addresses the critical lack of multilingual data in the ConvXAI domain. The datasets provide a resource for researchers to develop and evaluate models capable of understanding and responding to user queries in multiple languages. This is essential for broadening the accessibility and usability of ConvXAI systems.
    *   **Enabling Custom Input:**  The Compass dataset's focus on custom input extraction is significant because it allows for more adaptive and personalized ConvXAI systems. By enabling users to explore models with their own data, it enhances user engagement and promotes a deeper understanding of model behavior.
    *   **Improving Parsing Performance:**  The GMP approach shows promise in improving parsing accuracy in multilingual settings, which is crucial for the accurate interpretation of user intents. While not a radical breakthrough, the improvements in performance are meaningful and contribute to the development of more reliable ConvXAI systems.
    *   **Impact:** The datasets and the parsing approach can significantly impact the field of ConvXAI, enabling research towards more accessible, adaptable, and user-friendly explanation systems. The experiments and benchmarks provide a baseline for future research in multilingual ConvXAI.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a thorough evaluation of various models and approaches on the new datasets, offering valuable insights into their strengths and weaknesses.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper includes a detailed analysis of the translation quality and error patterns, which helps to understand the challenges and limitations of the current approaches.
    *   **Practical Contribution:** The creation of the datasets and the proposed parsing approach are practical contributions that can be directly used by other researchers in the field.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Machine Translation:** The reliance on machine translation for dataset creation introduces potential biases and inaccuracies. While human evaluation and correction are performed, the quality of the translations may still be a limiting factor.
    *   **Incremental Novelty of GMP:** The Guided Multi-prompt Parsing approach, while effective, is more of an incremental improvement over existing methods than a completely novel technique. The individual components (Guided Decoding and Multi-Prompt Parsing) are well-established. The contribution here is primarily their combination and adaptation for the multilingual context.
    *   **Limited Scope of Operations:** The operations implemented in Compass dataset are useful in context of XAI systems but can be limiting factor for future research and other ConvXAI systems.

* **Overall:** The paper is a solid contribution to the ConvXAI field. The dataset creation is a significant undertaking that addresses a real need, and the experimental results are useful for guiding future research. The limitations related to reliance on MT are inherent to the task, and the incremental nature of GMP does not diminish its practical value.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper's strength lies in addressing the limitations of current ConvXAI systems through the creation of valuable datasets and a parsing approach tailored for multilingual scenarios. While the core ideas behind GMP are not entirely new, the combination and adaptation of existing techniques, alongside the resources provided, warrants a good score. The significance of enabling custom input and providing resources for multi-lingual XAI is substantial. A score of 7.5 reflects the solid contributions and practical value, while acknowledging the incremental nature of some aspects of the work.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### TAIGen: Training-Free Adversarial Image Generation via Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.15020v1)
- **Authors**: Susim Roy, Anubhooti Jain, Mayank Vatsa, Richa Singh
- **Abstract**: Adversarial attacks from generative models often produce low-quality images and require substantial computational resources. Diffusion models, though capable of high-quality generation, typically need hundreds of sampling steps for adversarial generation. This paper introduces TAIGen, a training-free black-box method for efficient adversarial image generation. TAIGen produces adversarial examples using only 3-20 sampling steps from unconditional diffusion models. Our key finding is that perturbations injected during the mixing step interval achieve comparable attack effectiveness without processing all timesteps. We develop a selective RGB channel strategy that applies attention maps to the red channel while using GradCAM-guided perturbations on green and blue channels. This design preserves image structure while maximizing misclassification in target models. TAIGen maintains visual quality with PSNR above 30 dB across all tested datasets. On ImageNet with VGGNet as source, TAIGen achieves 70.6% success against ResNet, 80.8% against MNASNet, and 97.8% against ShuffleNet. The method generates adversarial examples 10x faster than existing diffusion-based attacks. Our method achieves the lowest robust accuracy, indicating it is the most impactful attack as the defense mechanism is least successful in purifying the images generated by TAIGen.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "TAIGen: Training-Free Adversarial Image Generation via Diffusion Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TAIGen, a novel training-free black-box method for generating adversarial examples using diffusion models. TAIGen stands out by achieving comparable attack effectiveness while significantly reducing the number of sampling steps required (3-20 steps) compared to traditional diffusion-based attacks. The key innovation lies in strategically injecting perturbations during the mixing step interval and employing a selective RGB channel strategy. This strategy utilizes attention maps on the red channel and GradCAM-guided perturbations on the green and blue channels, preserving image structure while maximizing misclassification in target models. The method demonstrates high visual quality (PSNR > 30 dB) and competitive attack success rates against various target models on ImageNet, CelebA-HQ, and CIFAR-10 datasets. Furthermore, TAIGen exhibits the lowest robust accuracy, indicating its effectiveness against defense mechanisms.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Efficiency:** The paper's primary strength is its efficiency. Reducing the number of diffusion steps from hundreds to just a few (3-20) is a significant practical improvement, making adversarial example generation more accessible.
*   **Training-Free:** The training-free nature of TAIGen simplifies its application, as it doesn't require retraining or fine-tuning the diffusion model for specific attack scenarios. This enhances its adaptability.
*   **Selective RGB Channel Strategy:**  The insights behind the channel selection strategy, leveraging attention maps and GradCAM, are interesting. The approach shows a nuanced understanding of how to effectively manipulate diffusion model latent spaces and achieve adversarial goals.
*   **Performance:** The experimental results showcase TAIGen's competitive attack success rates and high image quality (PSNR). The evaluation on various datasets and target models provides strong empirical support for the method's effectiveness.
*   **Robustness against Defenses:** Its performance in breaking through robust models, as indicated by the low robust accuracy, suggests this attack can be used to stress-test future defenses effectively.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited White-Box Performance:** The paper acknowledges TAIGen's weaker performance in white-box settings. While black-box attacks are often more relevant in real-world scenarios, understanding and addressing this limitation would strengthen the paper.
*   **Empirical Hyperparameter Tuning:** The selection of key timesteps (t_start and t_end) relies on empirical observation. Providing a more principled approach to determining these parameters or adapting them based on image characteristics would improve the method's robustness and generalizability.
*   **Ablation Study Depth:**  While the paper presents an ablation study, diving deeper into the contribution of each component and how they interplay could further refine understanding.

**Novelty and Significance:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel approach to adversarial example generation within diffusion models. The combination of mixing step interval perturbation, selective RGB channel manipulation, and the exploitation of attention maps offers a unique strategy that significantly improves efficiency. The focus on black-box attacks, training-free operation, and the observed robustness against defenses make this research highly relevant and applicable.

**Potential Impact:**

TAIGen could significantly influence the field by:

*   Encouraging more research into efficient adversarial attack methods for diffusion models.
*   Providing a practical tool for evaluating the robustness of deep learning models against black-box attacks.
*   Inspiring new defense mechanisms that target the specific vulnerabilities exploited by TAIGen.

**Justification for the Score:**

While the paper presents several important innovations, the limitations in white-box settings and the empirical hyperparameter tuning prevent a truly exceptional score. However, its high efficiency, compelling experimental results, and potential impact on the field warrant a high evaluation. TAIGen offers a significant step forward in training-free adversarial example generation.
